https://arxiv.org/pdf/2010.09216, compact closed categories in homotopical algebra. The idea is that compact closed categories are *-autonomous categories, whose tensor unit is the dualizing object, and admits the a left and right dual. The prototypical example is the category of finite dimensional vector spaces. Others include fg proj modules over a comm ring, or fg proj modules over a compact group. I was thinking that maybe duality fails over a noncommutative ring due to the failure of ergodicity or the semigroup condition, and commutativity or abelian results in some sort of compactness condition. Example: see figure, categories and start with free coonstruction, get coherently commutative monoidal categories, then end up with coherent compact closed categories. Folklore results and give aspects of duality.

Coexter system is a pair (G, S), group G, set of generators subject to relations only of the form (st)^m(s,t), m(s,s) = 1 for all s, m(s,t) more than or equal to 2, and m(s,t) = m(t,s), if no relation, set m(s,t) to infinity. https://iwoat.github.io/2018/slides/Ravenel.pdf  long slides by Hill-Hopkins-Ravenel on stable homotopy theory. AMAZING SET OF NOTES, MUST READ FOR STABLE HOMOTOPY THEORY. Spectrum is originally defined as sequence of pointed spaces or simplicial sets with structural maps of suspensions of the n-th cell onto the higher cell n+1. A map of spectra is a collection of pointed maps f_n compatible with the structural maps. Strict equivalence if each pointed map is a weak equivalence. Stable equivalence either requires (1) stable homotopy groups of spectra, require pi_* f to be isomorphism, or (2) functor of Sp to Sp where (Lambda X)_n is homotopy colimit meanning mapping telescope, then require Lambda f to be strict equivalence. Classically equivalent but fails in general cases. Differ in category of symmetric spectra of Hovey-Shipley-Smith. Quillen model categories, fibrant cofibrant replacement, Bousfield localization, enriched category theory. Quillen model category, three classes of morphisms, weak equivalences, fibrations, cofibrations, each of which include all isomorphisms, with bicompleteness, M has all small limits and colimits, prods, coprods, pullbacks, pushouts, M has initial and terminal objects. Bicompleteness make it feel like abelian categories, two out of three axioms, exact sequence, if any f, g, gf are weak equivalences, so is the third, retract of weak equivalence is again a weak equivalence. Lifting axiom, A to X, B to Y, for cofibration A to B, and trivial fibration X to Y, morphism h lifts. Diagram shifts for trivial cofibration and fibration on the other side. Factorisation axiom, morphisms can be functorially factored as trivial fibration * cofibration or fibration * trivial cofibration. Factorisation hardest to verify. As usual classical example is in category of compact generated weakly Hausdorff topological space, weak equivalences are maps inducing isomorphisms of homotopy groups, fibrations are Serre fibrations with right lifting prooperty, cofibrations are left lifting property. An object is cofibrant if unique map from initial object (empty set) to X is cofibration, object is fibration if unique map from X to point is fibration. All objects in T and Top are fibrant, cofibrant objects are CW complexes. This makes CW complexs very natural to consider. MC5, these maps from initial object to terminal object have factorisations (not unlike first iso theorem) for cofibrant and fibrant objects weakly equivalent to X. That is the point, CW approximations or topologic approximations. Kan recognition theorem give conditions for generating set of trivial cofibrations. CW complexes example, so easy to think of simplicial stuff? Too technical for talk for Kan recogition theorem. Bousfield localisation, basic idea, enlarge weak equivalences in some way, keep same class of cofibrations, define fibrations as right lifting property with repsect to newly defined trivial cofibrations. More weak equivalences, more trivial cofibrations, fewer fibrations, fewer fibrant objects, fibrant replacement functor becomes much more interesting. (Absolutely no clue what is mentioned). Consider topological space with usual Quillen model structure, 5 axioms, bireplacement, factorisation, 2-3, lifting, diagram shift for fibration cofibration. Choose an integer n, fibrant object are spaces with no homotopy above n, so fibrant replacement is n-th Postnikov section. Recall section means cross-section, choose prime p, map is weak equivalence inducing isomorphism in mod p homology, fibrant replacement functor is p-adic completion (if simply connected), now choose generalised homology theory, pick h_*f as isomorphism, SLIDE 121 GIVES CONCRETE EXAMPLES OF BOUSFIELD LOCALISATION. So anytime you need to impose isomorphisms on weak equivalences in homology, use Bousfield localisations. Pointed topological space or category of simpicial sets are standard examples. Enriched over V, morphism object, and composition morphisms of morphism objects with underlying categories. Simplicial category enriched over simplical sets. Symmetric monoidal category is closed if enriched over itself. Each pair of objects, exists internal hom object. Internal hom means closed. Definition of Yoneda spectrum, define spectrum as enriched functoir from indexing category J^N, spectrum was originally defined in terms of sequences of pointed spaces. Spectrum X is enriched functor from indexing category S^n-m n>=m, * otherwise to T, so spectrum can be thought of as a generalisation of a diagram! This is super insightful. Yoneda functor, T is symmetric monoidal, twist isomorphism, but morphism not in image of Yoneda spectrum. No twist isomorphism in J^N. Category of pointed topological space T is closed symmetric monoidal, indexing category is symmetric monoidal and enriched over T. Day Convolution comes into play. https://ncatlab.org/nlab/files/Makkai-GabrielUlmerDuality.pdf  Gabriel-Ulmer duality, theorem: (1) LEX is a category whose objects are 0 cells or categories with finite limits, 1 cells are functors preserving finite limits, 2-cells are all natural transformations, LFC is a category with smal limits and filtered colimits. Sets with 2 adjunction between LEX^op to LFC, C in LEX, C* in LEX(C; Set), where counit eps_C from C to C^*#, Gabriel Ulmer duality gives equivalence of category. Explained from slides 1 to 4;

https://www.math.ias.edu/~lurie/278xnotes/Lecture10-Giraud.pdf  Giraud's axioms as explained by Jacob Lurie: (1) Theorem 3 is striking, X is a category, and X is a topos iff there is a small category C and a fully faithful embedding X into Fun(C^op, Set), admitting a left adjoint where L preserves finite limits, so X is not a canonical Yoneda self embedding, normally the Yoneda embedding is a functor C -> Func(C^op, inftyGrpd), basically it says X can be extended to Yoneda embedding whose left adjoint preserves finite limits; A topos is a category that can be extended to a Yoneda embedding whose left adjoint preserves finite limits.

https://mathoverflow.net/questions/293031/gabriel-ulmer-duality-for-infty-categories, Denis Nardin's proof of Gabriel-Ulmer duality: (1) describe the Gabriel-Ulmer duality in terms of infinity categories; (2) factor it into 3 fucnctors, ^op, Ind(-),  and equiv, the first functor sends idempotent complete, finitely comnplete infity-category to its opposite, this is op or equiv of infinity 2 categories, second is inverse of equivalence picking kappa = omega, using HTT.5.5.7.8, third is HTT.5.5.3.4 sends it to itself, sends every functor to right adjoint, this is a restriction. All three functors are equivalences; A Gabriel-Ulmer duality in infinity categories, is a contravariant ind-completion of the infinity category of finitely complete and idempotent complete infinity categories (i.e. finite limits with idempotent logic) and the infinity category of locally finitely presentable infinity categories.

https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.11967, duality of clans. (1) page 2 gives the best description of Gabriel-Ulmer duality, it is a duality of theories and models. Small finite limit categories are viewed as theories, so for theory T, T-Mod is locally finitely presentable. FL is small finite limit categories, LFP is locally finitely presentable categories. Models are left exact (preserve finite limits) functors of essentiallly algebraic theories (has all finite limits). A Gabriel Ulmer duality is a functorial equivalance of small finite limit categories, which are theories, and locally finite presentable categories, which means we can describe them finitely.

https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0509111, Tannaka family of flat connections: (1) section 2 on page 5 onwards describes a construction of Tannaka categories via the fibre functors; (2) the easiest example, let f from X to Spec(K) be smooth, geometrically connected, variety over a field of char 0, K contains k is a field extension, and x in X(K) is a rational point. There is the Tannaka category of bundles C(X/K), objects are bundles (i.e. locally free coherent modules [fg and fg submodules is fp]) these are not neutral Tannakian categories, bundles are a form of representation theory, so this is Tannakian; A Tannkian category is a category with Tannaka duality or a fibre functor such that there is a functor of points from the Tannakian category to points a etale fundamental group of the Tannakian category that is omega compact in finite sets to be an equivalence of categories.

https://arxiv.org/pdf/1204.5787, Tannaka duality over ring spectra, gives the best explanation of etale pi 1 using Tannaka's story: (1) history given in page 2, Galois categories are categories C with a fiber functor such that C is equivalent to the category of representations of a profinite group then we have a statement of C -> pi_1(C, omega)-FSet for finite set endowed with an action of pi_1(C, omega) or a fundamental group over a base point an equivalent of categories known as Galois duality, this is the etale fundamental group!; (2) Tannaka duality (neutralised) is clearly explained for T an algebraic fundamental group (NOT JUST A CATEGORY). A neutral Tannaka duality is a functor T -> Rep(pi(T, omega)^alg) from k-Tannakian category T, to representations of the etale fundamental group of affine group schemes as an equivalence of categories, where pi(T, omega)^alg is an affine group scheme.

Kraft inequality is because the main observation is a set of prefix free binary code words corresponds to binary tree and oriented paths from root to one of the leaves, binary tree, used induction over subtree. Canonical example is category of open sets, objects are open sets, arrows are inclusions, collection of open covers, fibred products of inclusions are intersection. Jointly surjective if set theoretic union of images is equal to U. small Zariski site is category, full subcategory of Sch/X of objects U -> X that are open immersions equipped with a Grothendieck topology by defining a cover to be joint surjective set fo open embeddings. Big Zariski site is full (Sch/X), there is small etale versions, full subcategory of SchX of objects U -> X that are etale (flat and unramified) and locally of finite presentation, equipped with a Grothendieck topology by defining a cover to be a jointly surjective set of etale morphisms. https://pbelmans.ncag.info/notes/spectral-sequences-examples.pdf  simplest notes of spectral sequences. Krull's ideal theorem intersecting with hypersurface drops at most dim 1, let A be a ring, M be module over A, regular sequence of x_n if (x_1, ... x_n)M is not equal to M, x_i is a nonzero diviosr in M/(x_1,... x_i-1) for all i in 1,...n. Most classical regular sequence is A = k[x,y,z] field, M = A, describe a point using x,y,z. Check that x, y(1-x), z(1-x) is a regular sequence. Order of regular sequence is important, for local rings permutations of regular sequences are again regular sequences. One now sees with analogy of regular lcoal rings, if maximal ideal is a way of generating the maximal ideal with minimal number of generators such that dim A = n, then A is regular, regular local ring of Krull dimension is generated by regular sequence in ideals of same dimension, permutations of dimensions appear. Where does S_n appear in the theory of regular sequences? Depth can be understood. Depth of an ideal such that IM != M (prevent Nakayama's nonzeroness), for M be fg module over A, I ideal of A, A be ring, depth of an ideal with respect to M is the minimum {i such that there is nontrivial Ext group Ext^i_A(A/I, M) != 0}. Depth is known as homological codimension. Rees has this giving it geometry. Consider local ring (A,m), M be fg A-module all maximal regular sequences, such that x_i in m have depth length = depth_m(M), depth measures how much we can cut down things using hypersurfaces, Krull's ideal theorem means cut down intersection 1, drop dim 1. Homological dimension is the correct notion of dimension in the absence of regular local rings. This yields bound depth_m(A) <= dim A, dim A is Krull dimension associated with dimension of reg sequences, depth_m(A) is homological dimension. Inequality is only equal for Cohen-Macaulay rings. Full ring is Cohen-Macualay if localisation is CM for all prime ideal in Speec A. Krull dimension measures things from bottom to top: we start from the zero-dimensional parts and go up. Depth on the other hand is characterised by Rees in terms of regular sequences: these cut the whole ring down using hypersurfaces. So Cohen–Macaulay rings are those rings for which it doesn’t matter in which way we measure things: depth and height are complementary.

https://legacy-www.math.harvard.edu/theses/senior/lehner/lehner.pdf  Riehl all concepts are Kan extensions. (1) a Kan extension is an extension of the preimage category from a base category (as a natural transformation) respecting representables, a left Kan extension is the left adjoint, a right Kan extension is a right adjoint. a Kan lift is an lift to the preimage category from an embedding to a base category respecting representables. If the base category is terminal, left Kan extensions turn the preimage extension into a colimit over a terminal base, right Kan extensions turns the preimage embedded into a limit over a terminal base. A functor can be thought of as a Kan extension with a represented base category, the left Kan extensions are left adjoints, right Kan extensions are right adjoints.

http://wwwf.imperial.ac.uk/~buzzard/xena/pdfs/liquid_tensor_experiment.pdf  Liquid tensor experiment, I have finished reading this, gives a very rapid introduction to the topic. (1) key theorem, let p and p' be the norm order in the unit interval of reals, let S be a profinite set or an countable inverse limit of finite sets; let V be a p-Banach space, let M_p'(S) be the space of p' measures on S, then the Ext-group in condensed abelian groups of the space of p' measures on profinite set S for i greater than or equal to 1 vanish; (2) compact generated weak Hausdorff spaces embed fully into condensed sets with internal homs i.e. T -> Hom(X,Y) is functorial to T x X -> Y or condensed sets are locally cartesian closed, or form a topos up to kappa-smallness page 1; (3) motivation is p-adic annulus T in C_p, modulus T = 1, extra p-power roots, infinite tower of covers, pass to limit object as a perfectoid space to have tilting to relate to characteristic p, the main idea of the pro-etale site; (4) condensed sets are sheaves on the pro-etale site of a point, covers are given by finite collections of jointly surjective maps; (5) motivation is compact Hausdorff spaces serve as spaces that admit surjections from a finite set i.e. fibres are cardinality at least 1; one can then recover T as the quotient of S by R in the diagonal product S * S; (6) decimal expansions form a surjection from a profinite set onto the interval which is a diaognal product of {0,1,2,...9}, so the interval is a quotient of a profinite set; (7) exact sequence of discrete to typical topology has trivial kernel and cokernel in TopAb, but quotient of continuous maps valued in R mod out by locally constant maps valued in R; (8) compact proj generators are extremelly disconnected profinite sets, and is tied to axaiom of choice, non-principle ultrafilters; one can do homological algebra; (9) locally compact abelian groups in condensed abelian groups as a category has trivial ext groups for i >= 2, nontrivial Ext^1 which agree with the category of locally compact abelian groups, normal homs or Ext^0 are usual continuous maps, and is prove by Breen-Deligne resolution; (10) completions to solidity is the key idea, a condensed abelian group is solid if for any profinite set S (countable cofiltered limit of discrete space) any map from S to M (M is the base, S is the fibration) extends uniquely (Kan extension like) to Z[S]^solid, where Z[S] solid is the inverse limit of Z[S_i]. This is the hom of continuous maps from S to Z, or the Z-valued measures on S, any maps gives a unique map sending a measure to the integral over the measure, there is a map of Dirac measures; (11) discrete groups are solids, limits, colimits, internal homs, tensor products have to be resolidified, one can do p-adic functional analysis; (12) a condensed liquid space is p-liquid if for any profinite set S, and for any p' < p, any map admits a extension of measures over p' for S to V, one cannot naively use solidification Z[S] for the reals since reals have solification 0, directly take union of compact Hausdorff spaces of signed Radon measures as a condensed abelian group, so liquidification is for the case of solidification of R; (13) reals are not locally profinite, bounds on real numbers leads to instability in addition;

https://people.mpim-bonn.mpg.de/scholze/Complex.pdf  Complex Geometry by Peter Scholze and Dustin Clausen, I need to reread this again with more background in analytic geometry, functional analysis, Banach and Stein stuff, and background in condensed math from the previous two sets of notes: (1) page 6, a sheaf of holomorphic functions has transition maps on open discs convergent power series with transition maps compatible with evaluation at points, so holomorphicity can be checked locally, once locally defined it gives a global convergent Taylor series expansion with vanishing higher sheaf cohomology; (2) page 7, existence of sheaf of holomorphics come from Cauchy's integral formula, the analyticity of holomorphics as special smooth functions, use bump functions to see sheaf vanishes on higher cohomology; (3) idea of analytic ring is topological ring with topological A module such that A pullbacks from the completion of N with infinity extends uniquely to a pullback to summable sequences, more concretly S is profinite, the A-module is cA-valued measures on S, these sequences are sending elements to measures in the A-module, with a unique extension to an integral, page 8; (4) N and infinity is in general profinite set with a countable inverse limit, page 8; (5) an anlytic ring is a topological ring A, with a module of measures as a topological A-module (example Radon-measures) for any profinite set (which are Dirac deltas), this analogies fails due to R being funky, page 8. (6) topological abelian groups do not form an abelian kernel, since the map from the discrete topology to the natural topology has trivial kernel, cokernel and is not an isomorphism; (7) condensed mathematics is motivated by the exact sequence of the discrete topology to the natural topology, which forms a quotient of continuous functions quotiented out by the local constant functions, where this quotient is a condensed abelian group functor; (8) page 10 gives a preliminary definition, analytic rings are condensed rings with a condensed A-module of measures for any test space a profinite set, with maps from profinite sets to condensed A-module of vector space "Dirac measures" with a full subcategory of M_A-completed condensed A-modules admitting a unique extension as an abelian subcategory or a "heart" of Cond(A), stable under extensions; (9) page 11 gives motivation to promote the sheaf of holomorphic functions to an abelian tensor category; (10) Inverse limits of finite discrete spaces with a basis of clopen sets (used compact Hausdorff here) form a category of compact Hausdorff totally disconnected topological spaces, with a canonical choice for the inverse limit disgram to be indexed by set of disjoint union decompositions into finitely many nonempty clopen sets, with partial order given by refinement, this canonical index diagram is cofilterd, so this inverse limit is cofiltered inverse limit of finite sets, page 11; (11) a covering is an induced map from a cofinite disjoint union of maps from a common codomain lifted to a full cofiltered inverse limit of finite sets is surjective. This is called the kappa-restricted finitary Grothendieck topology on the category of profinite sets, or a cofiltered kappa-small (card less than kappa) inverse limit of finite sets; (12) on page 12, an element in the category of kappa-condensed set is a contravariant functor of points of restricted kappa-small disjoint union of joint surjective maps, with the contravariances taking finite collections of Prof^op_kappa (kappa small boolean algebras) to finite products; on page 13 pullback functors from a category with a base of large cardinals exist, the inclusion on profinite sets exists as Yoneda class of cardinals, the functor is fully faithful under a cofinal class of cardinals; (13) a quasicompact condensed sets is that for all collections of maps of condensed sets, for all surjections from a base of disjoint opens to a condensed set, there exists a finite indexing subset for this surjection, every profinite set is quasicompact, so the existence of the surjection and a profinite set for the base of the surjection, this gives a cleaner definition; (14) a condensed set is quasicompact if and only if it is the quotient of a profinite set; (15) non-Hausdorff behaviour is ruled out by qusiseparatedness, a quasiseparated condensed set if the diagonal map to the product is quasicompact, form a quasicompact product of profinite set as a condensed set; (16) subcategory of quasicompact quasiseparated condensed sets are basically compact Hausdorff spaces (drop locally), quotietns of profintite sets by closed equivalent relations; (17) a condensed set X is projective if for all other condensed sets Y, the induced surjective pushforward to the condensed set as a base (or target) admits a section i.e. Y ->> X surjection has a section; (18) for every condensed set S, the Stone Cech compactification of the profinite set is a projective condensed set that admits a induced surjective pushforward on the condensed set as a base. The Stone Cech compactification serves as a universal generator for the category of condensed sets; (18) a condensed abelian gbroup is an abelian group object or a sheaf of abelian group of kappa-small cofiltered inverse limits of finite sets, this forms the abelian category. Since the Stone-Cech construction gives enough quasicompact projective profinite objects, there is better exactness; (19) free condensed abelian group on quasicompact projective objects is a compact projective object on the category of ocondensed abelian groups, and is a representable hom functor to the category of abelian groups from condensed abelian groups. (20) liquid vector spaces are condensed abelian groups that are complete which means infinite sums exist and are unique, with integrals of continuous functions as measures; (21) a measure assigns a Borel subset a real number with countable additivity and bounded variation, specify measures for clopen subsets of profinte sets with finite additivity and bounded variation, the signed Radon measures as condensed ring modules being maps from formed from disjoint unions of maps of clopen sets to the real line with zero measure, finite additivity with bounded variation, alternatively one can think of the signed Radon measures as a condensed ring module or the disjoint union of a filtered inverse limit as a embedding to the filtered inverse limit of R^oplus S_i (finite dim vector space over R induced by the disjoint union of profinite sets), i.e. the disjoint union of closed balls of bounded variation of radius C centred at the origin with respect to the Manhattan norm on R^oplus S_i. One can switch the Manhatten norm to any other norm, but p < 1 means closed discs are no longer complex, we have nonconvex functional analysis; (22) page 18 gives signed Radon measures as a module of condensed rings which are the unions of filtered inverse limits of discrete sets of bounded l^p variation that have kappa-small cardinality; (23) IMPORTANT, let V be a condensed abelian group with fixed p for norm, V is p-liquid if for every condensed set and direct images of condensed sets into the condensed anelian group, there is a unique map of condensed abelian groups from the signed Radon measure as a condensed ring module with bounded variation under norm < p which is the disjoint union of closed balls centred at the origin for all l^p norms less than p, such that this unique map is the unique extension of the map of condensed sets into the map of condensed abelian groups. I call this the unique p-liquid map extension. https://arxiv.org/pdf/1001.2282, algebraic K-theory is something which is defined e.g. for any presentable stable infinity category. It inverts Morita equivalences, preserves filtered colimits in the infinity categorical sense, and sends exact sequences to cofiber sequences any functor that has these properties is a so called localizing invariant, and there is a universal such functor U_loc with target the stable infinity category of noncommutative motives. Extend this,  "continuous K-theory" in terms of large localizing invariants for dualizable stable infinity categories (where you no longer have generation by small categories of compact projective objects) and there is a similar universal characterization by Efimov and Clausen and others, this perspective is clarifying because there are loads of other localizing invariants which one also considers in practice (not least because they are easier to compute than algebraic K-theory) and this universal property automatically produces traces from algebraic K-theory to any other localizing invariant.

https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/9901053, q-deform a construction of Kazhdan and Kostant from 1970's which produces quantum Toda Hamiltonians by considering the action of Casimirs of a simple Lie algebra on Whittaker functions on the corresponding Lie group. We also give the affine analog of this generalization. This is done by extending the notion of a Whittaker function to quantum groups and quantum affine algebras. We compute the q-deformed Toda Hamiltonians for Lie algebras of type A and show that they coincide with those known in the theory of integrable systems.

https://www.claymath.org/library/academy/LectureNotes05/Lodaypaper.pdf  survey of some of the nice properties of the associahedron (also called Stasheff polytope) from several points of views: topological, geometrical, combinatorial and algebraic, this is very good paper worth working out in all concrete details. (1) to define associahedron, it is in one to one correspondence of polytope with parenthesizing of 0 to n+1 words i.e. brackets; (2) to define it as a polytope it is isomorphic to Stasheff polytope; (3) to classify spaces homotopically equivalent to loop spaces, one define partial order on Stasheff polytope then read off the eucldiean realisation; (4) to use algebra, one can endow the vector space of planar binary trees with associative algebra using a partial order with a dendriform algebra structures; (5) to construct the associatehedron one enlarges over conics, the coordinates of the edges are important and gives it its shape; (6) to have Stasheff complex, one consider elementary homotopies of putting brackets; (7) to define the covering order of the partial order of the Stasheff polytope, one puts a Tamari order; (8) to define a vertices with permutations, one defines a permutohedron; (9) to define the order of the permutohedron, one uses the weak bruhat order; (10) to define shuffles, one uses the faces of the permutohedron; (11) to study formal power series by replacing integers n with planar binary trees, one use the dendriform series and the dendriform algebra of a Stasheff polytope; (12) to define a commutative dendriform algebra, one defines the Zinbiel algebras; (13) to correspond to Zinbiel algebras, one correspond this to commutative algebras, to correspond to associative algebras, one looks at Lie algebras; (14) to study the trefoil knot, one can relate it to the associahedron by the path of a centre of a square; (15) to define real points of M_0(R) or moduli space of n labeled puncutres of Riemann spheres, one tiles with associahedrons;

https://chrisjelliott.github.io/Motivic_L_functions.pdf  exposition on motivic L functions, good paper it is very short and explains a lot, I did not understand the last part though. (1) to count points of varieties of finite fields, one appeal to the Weil conjectures; (2) to prove for Weil conjectures for all curgves and abelian varieties, Weil sued the Tate-module as a linearisation to reduce to linear algebra; (3) to understand linearisations of an algerbaic variety, one produces a number of cohomology theories that remember as much structure as possible, so one develop etale cohomology one can prove the Weil conjectures as per Deligne; (4) to identify the definition of cohomology theories on topology, one must use the Eilenberg Steenrod axioms; (5) to define cohomology theories in algebraic theories, one must resort to thinking about the Weil cohomology theory, and that it does not exist over the rational numbers; (6) to prove that there is no Weil cohomology theory when the field is Q, one uses a super singualr elliptic curve; (7) to define a universal cohomology theory, one will need a theory of motives; (8) to define an intersection product, one enlarges the homset of the category of smooth projective varieties and then choosing rational equivalence to have a Chow ring, then enlarge the homset to include all former morphisms and define the pushforward as the composition law to the intersection product to X x Y x Z and then pullback to X x Z, forming a category Corr(K); (9) to make Corr(K) abelian, one uses idempotent completion or formally splitting idempotents to ensure they have kernels and cokerenls aka Corr(K) is an abelian category; (10) to create the category of pure motives, one must have every Weil cohomology functor factor through realisations to preserve the grading, and formally adjoin inverses tho the Lefschetz motives to produce the category of pure motives; (11) to generalise the zeta function, one defines an L-function which is a holomorphic ufnction defined in C that encodes number theory or scheme of finite type or a motive; (12) to prove that there are infintiely many primes, one can use the fact that zeta has a pole at s = 1, and the product formula of primes; (13) to understand an L-function, one epxresses it as an Euler product, then it has factors from the arithmetic behaviour, then prove theorems on meromorphic continuation and the funcitonal equation; (14) to generalise zeta functions from primes to prime ideals, one will write down the Dedekind zeta functions to get a product formula using a generalisation of the absolute norm of an ideal; (15) to calculate the residue of the pole at s = 1 akin to infinitude of the primes, Hecke proived the analytic class number formula with the classical regulator; (16) to encode the density of the roots of untiy, one defines the regulator and uses Dirichlet's unit theorem; (17) to concretely understand a motivic decomposition of L-function, one studies the basic example of Dedekind zeta functions; (18) to define analogue of primes of number ring for scheme of finite type over Z, one uses closed points to generalise norms of ideals; (19) to fill in missing factors by choosing a finite set of primes S, one needs to study elliptic curves to understand the bad reduction resulting in the incomplete zeta function; (20) to count points over finite fields of prime powers, one uses the Lefschetz trace formula; (21) to make the zet afunction independent of the choice of model E, one must define the inertia group at a prime p to be a kernel of a homomorphism of galois groups, then one can write the zeta factor in a model independent way which is a huge mess; (22) to generalise taking L-functions of Galois representations, one define the Artin L-functions; (23) to define the full subcategory of motives generated by zero dimensional objects, one can find it to be equivalent to the category of Galois representation with the motive mapping to the regular representation of Gal(K/Q);

Suppose we have a generalised homology theory, this can be represented by a spectrum E. We say a spectrum Z is E-local if, whenever, f : A -> B is an E_*-equivalence, that is a map inducing an isomorphism E_*A -> E_*B, then induced map f* : [B,Z] -> [A,Z] is also an isomorphism. With this definition, one can define a coaugmented functor L_E such that for any spectrum X, L_E X is E-local, map X -> L_EX is an E_* equivalence. E and X are both connective, L_E X can be described in arithmetic terms, either obtained from X by inverting some set of primes, or it is p-adic completion of single prime, so connectivity means somehow universal stuff and you can take simple algebraic constructions to do the Bousfield localisation? So Bousfield localisation in 1979 is a generalisatioon of inverting set of primes or p-adic completion. Hypothetical spectrum M_n can be constructed from inductively constructed N_n using Bousfield localisation, logical choice for E appeared to be Johnson-Wilson spectrum E(n), closely related to fancier Morava spectrum E_n. Both lead to the same localisation functor. Two spectra or Bousfield equivalent, if they have the same class of acyclic spectra that is E_* C = 0 and E_*'C = 0. The Bousfield equivalence class is denoted by <E>. Information, {E} >= {F} if E_* gives at least as much informations as F_*, maximal Bousfield class is the sphere spectrum, minimal one is that of a point. Wedges and smash products of Bousfield classes are well defined (as one can check). Formal properties of Bousfield classes include W -> X -> Y -> Sum W is a cofiber sequence, <X> <= <W> smash <Y> meaning f is smash nilpotent: f^{^k} : Y^{^k} -> (Sum W)^{^k} is null for some k. For self-map, denote its cofiber, find mapping telescope. Some Bousfield equivalences, sphere spectrum is <SQ> wedge wedge for all prime p <S/p>, this is the prototype example, SQ is rational Moore spectrum, S/p is mod p Moore spectrum. Another example is <BP> >= <H/p> wedge wedge all positive <K(n)>, so <BP> as the graded ring as a result of the formal group llaw contains as much information as <H/p> which is the mod p Eilenberg-Maclane spectrum and full wedge <K(n)>, K(n) is the Morava K-theory. <E(n)> = <E_n> = wedge K(i), smash product of any two wedge summands on the right is contractible (not sure what this means). Localisation functor is determined by Bousfield class <E>. When is map from X to L_infty X an equivalence, if is say X is harmonic, these are the chromatic tower of a p-local spectrum. L_infty X is harmonic localisation. It is dissonant if L_infty X equivalent to point means Morava K-theory K(n)_* X = 0. It follows from definitions that there are no essential maps from a dissonant spectrum to a harmonic one. Ravenel paper, every p-local finite spectrum is harmonic, p-local connective spectrum X is harmonic when BP_* X has finite projective dim as BP_*-module, mod p Eilenberg-Maclane spectrum H/p is dissonant. (3) to formulate the theory of local rings, one can define 0 != 1, and inv(x) or inv(1-x) where inv means exist 1 = xy; (4) to define Henselian in coherent way, one can describe strictly Henselian instead; (4) to define Zariski spectrum in a logical way, define it as universal support of R to Z(R), where supports are maps R to M to bunded distributive lattice M satisfying ideal axioms; (5) to define unramifiable, one means the polynomial has a simple root in some extension of the residue field; (6) to define Azumaya algebras, it is algebra that is projective of finite type, and tensor prod A otimes A^op to End_k(A) be isomoprhism; (7) to give constructive proofs in Azumaya algebra theory, one use tree inductions.

https://sites.math.rutgers.edu/~ctw/quotients.pdf  notion of quotient in geometric invariant theory introduced by Mumford, good set of notes in revising Lie group theory. (1) finite dimensional versions of Harder-Narasimahan and Jordan-Holder filtrations, one technique can be using convexity of Kempf-Ness functions; (2) moment polytope computation, one can use some sort of qantisation; (3) time one flow at the identiy, use exponential map of the Lie algebra (which is left invariant vector field Lie action) on the Lie group where group multiplication is a smooth math. (4) make Lie group a vector space on it, define left action as smooth map with associativity and identiy, then one has K-manifold, and if works like a linear algebra, then it is K-equivariant; (5) remember sign of Lie bracket, due to left invariant vector fields, these generate right action, while actions are defined on the left; (6) to stabilise a fixed point x, define the stabiliser and the orbit, where orbit is constant is stabiliser; (7) to define de Rham operators, one needs graded derivations or a product rule on D(a0a1) = D(a0a1) + (-1)^d|a0| a0 D(a1); (8) to distinguish complex linear transformations from classical groups of linear symplectomorphisms, sympletic manifold with sympletic closed non-degenerate two form defined; (9) to define conserved quantity, one defines symmetries of a Hamiltonian dynamical system with energy function, and Noether's theroem; (10) to remove restriction on symplectomorphisms since must be same direction, one uses a Lagrangian correspondence or Lagrangian submanifolds to remove the dimension restriction; (11) to generalise symplectic geometry, one defines Poisson geometry but not compatible with Lagrangian correspondences as morphisms; (12) to define orgdinary momentum in symplectic manifold, one defines a moment map using Hamiltonian maps which lifts to equivariant map of Lie algebras and comoment maps; (13) to calculate area of the two sphere, one can do a moment map calculation this can be done to obtain the area of the cylinder between the two z-values; (14) to do linear algebra, exterior algebra, pullbacks, interior products, one defines a Hamiltonian K-manifold; (15) to generalise Darboux theorem, one use Morse theory to define an equivariantly closed differential form, this is introduced in Atiyah and Bott; (16) to interpret linearisastion of an action, the moment map can be thought of in terms of an infinitesimal linearisation or a lift to a vector bundle; (17) to ensure that quotients are well defined, where free actions means symplectic structure is admitted, one defines symplectic quotient as preimage of moment map on 0, quotiented out by the Hamiltonian K-manifold; (18) to give an example of symplectic quotient; the product of unit two spheres with diagonal action of K = SO(3) is the moduli space of closed n-gons with lengths rescaled by lambda or eigenvalues, where the moment maps sends each point to some sort of trace; (19) to describe compatibility with complex structure, one has Kahler manifolds see page 18; (18) orbits of points and stabilisers of points are a thing on page  3; (19) a Hamiltonian dynamical system has a sympletic manifold X and an energy function H in C^\infty(X), if K is another function like component of engular moment, {K, H} = 0L_K^# H = L_H# K, H is invariant under the flow generated by K# iff K is conserved in time, this is the content of Noether's theorem, for avery symmetry of a Hamiltonian system, there is a conserved quantity; (19) sums, products and duals are natural on sympletic manifolds, symplectomorphisms must have same dimension.

https://srln.se/presentation.pdf  notes on Galois theory with an introduction to etale pi 1, extremely amazing introduction to Galois categories and etale pi 1: (1) to prove the fundamental theorem of Galois theory as a fucntor: (1) one has the opposite category of finite etale k-algebra, etale is isomorphic to finite product of separable extensions K_i of k, then the opposite category to finite left Gal(k)-sets taking A to hom_k(A, k_s) so this is an antiequivalence of categories; (2) to choose an absolute Galois group, one must choose a separable closure; (3) to define the fundamental theorem of Galois theory, one must use limits of finite etale algebras or union of all finite separable extensions; (4) to study the fundamental group and its action, one must study the monodromy action of pi_1(X, x) acting on the fbre preimage(x), this will be a path from y to another point in the fibre over X, defining a permutation of the fibre; (5) to study topology of Galois theory,one can have the main theorem of covering theory where coverings of topological space X is equivalent to left pi_1(X,x)-sets; restrict to finite covers means these are continuous left profinite completed left pi_1(X,x) sets; (6) to define Galois categories, one uses the fundamental covering tehory with a functor called the fibre functor lifting the category to G-sets; (7) to define the etale fundamental group, one defines etale_pi^1(X, bar x) to be G or automorphisms of the fibre functor; (8) to study anabelian schemes, they must be reconstructibel from their fundamental group under various definitions of smoothness, this is the content of anabelian geometry; (9) to define Galois categories, these are continuous left G-sets on a profinite group, then G is the fundamental group of the Galois category, with a fibre lifting from G. = Aut(Fibre functor) from C to G-sets;

Polyhedra is homemorphic to sphere, pick 2 - 2g for Euler characteristic. Attach algebraic invariants to topological spaces. Homology groups. Simplicial complex has vertices and collection of finite subsets called simplices. Simplex gets field in, so strangely you just need rule is if S in collection and S' in S then S' in collection, the other rule is singletons {v} in S. Simplicial complexes allow you to attach a topology to things which by themselves have no topology. Euler's formula still applies. Define CW complex as some sort of viltration X = union of all filtration elements, X^0 is discrete set, X^n is inductively obtined by a bunch of n-cels X^n = X^n-1 gluing coproduct of D_^n disks, f_a is map S^n-1 -> X^n-1. X has the weak topology of the {X^n}, map out of X is continuous if and only if restrictions to X^n are continuous, X^n is the n-skeleton. Think of it is decompositions. Theorem, any manifold is CW-complex, using R^n as the n-skeleton. RP^n is a CW complex, attach upper hemisphere and glue boundary to RP^n-1, nontrivial CW complex. Riemann spheres can be constructed via CW complexes as well.

(5) to understand the splitting of the J-homomorphism, one needed to understand Spin cobordism i.e. Bott periodicity and the 10 fold way; (6) to study the group of cobordism classes, one is led to investigate Thom spectra as well as MO or the unoriented cobordism, MU is the unitary or complex cobordism; (7) to generalise the tensor product of abelian groups, the smash product was invented and looks like some sort of addition; (8) to have a universal theory of Chern classes for complex vector bundles, MU is the universal theory; (9) to approximate a space whose stable or unstable homotopy groups are known, one uses the Adam's spectral sequence; (10) to refine rings to ring spectra, topological modular forms is used to refine the theory of elliptic curves and modular forms, recall from Lurie that the top 5 facts of algebraic geometry, elliptic curves form a group with infinity as an identity; (11) to uniquely find a homotopy fixed point spectrum, one needs to think up a ring of topological modular forms, whose surjectivity of the map is proved by Hopkins-Mahowald; (12) to understand the cell structure of complex projective space, one can compute the cohomology of spectra for CP^\infty valued in Z; A topos is a category rich enough to support internal language. A ring is local iff precisely one maximal ideal. BEST DEFINITION OF LOCAL, a homomorphism is local iff it reflects invertibility. http://web.science.mq.edu.au/~street/CT90Como.pdf  Joyal Street Tannakian, recall classical Pontryagin duality, character is continuous homomorphism from G -> T, multiplicative group of modulus 1, G^dual given topology of uniform convergence of compact subsets, G^dual is locally compact, with inner product acting as inner hom from G^dual * G -> T (looks like inner hom?) with canonical homomorphism of double duals, Pontrjagin duality states this homomorphism is isomorphsim of topological groups. Fourier transform can be defined as F : L^1(G) cap L^2(G) -> L^2(G^dual), with integral f(x) <s, x> dx. Plancherel's theorem, with correct normalisation of Haar measure on G^dual f to Ff extends uniquely to an isometry, measure associated to dx, so that total mass of G = 1. Note, failure of general duallity for noncommutative locally compact groups, simple algebraic groups, duality fails badly.

Operad, category of operators is category with functor to pointed finite sets, objects are lists, morphisms are single morphism of skeleton and a multimorphism from lists of objects to single object in operad, composition comes from composition in operad. So operads are functors from category of operators valued in pointed finite sets. Feels very like functor of categories, functor of points. Idea is to have collection of morphisms become equivalence under the smash product. Ordinary categories have a class of maps of weak equivalences, localisation is universal among functors sending maps inW to isomorphism, the new category is unique up to unique isomorphism. The basic example is projection being a weak equivalence, in localisation, homotopic maps are equal, so localisation factors through th ehomotopy cateogry hS. The homotopy category has a large enough special objects, localisation is done by forcibly moving objects into this subcategory. The second example is in th ectegory of nonnegatively grade cochain complexes of modules over a commutative ring, W is a the class of quasiisomorphisms, there is an injective module of complexes as a subcategory The composite functor to the localisation is an equivalence of categories. These examples are worth revisiting laters. Definition of locality, SUPER IMPORTANT: let S be a class of morphisms in A, an object in Y is S-local if for all f : A -> B, the map of homs Hom_A(B,Y) -> Hom_A(A,Y) is a bijection, so it is local in the class of morphisms, since homsets can be formed on the level of domain and codomain of maps f, the L^S(A) is a full subcategory of S-local objects, if there is only one map f (or even a canonical map), then this property is f-local. Special case of localisation is when maps in S map to a terminal object. Let S be a class of morphisms mapping to the terminal object, the localisation is the nullification of the objects, a map is es-equivalent if for all S-local objects Y, the Hom_A(B,Y) -> Hom_A(A,Y) is a bijection, the class of S-equivalences contains S by definition. A map X -> Y is an S-localisation, if it is an S-equivalence and Y is local, and under these conditions we say X has an S-localisations. If all objects in category A have S-localisations, we say A has S-localisations. Example: class of maps f: N -> Z in category of monoids, monoids M is f-local if monoid hom N -> M extends to hom Z -> M, so every element in M has an inverse, f-local monoids are groups, this is the natural transformation to its group completion. Localisation comes from making sure that there is a unique maximal ideal, focusing on a local ring. Localisations are left adjoints to the inclusion of a subcategory. Localisation can also be done using mapping spaces. Invert inclusion of S^n -> D^n+1, to have uniqueness for filling maps from n-spheres, need existence for filling maps from (n+1)-spheres. Small object argument to construct Bousfield localisations.

Lagrange's theorems has the following corrolaries, group of order n can be generated by at most log_2(n) elements. This argument works by induction on cosets, minimal prime being 2.s The number of nonisomorphic groups is at most n^{n log_2 n}. This follows by Cayley's theorem, use the fact that symmetric group has order n!. Best possible is n^{c (log n)^2} by classification of finite simple groups. Group actions follow from closure and identity axioms, inverse axioms not necessary. Equivalence classes of residue classes form orbits of the action. Stabilises means trivial action on the element. Action is transitive if only one orbit. Any action can be decomposite to transitive action, this is the first structure theorem for group actions. Second structure theorem implies that transitive action is isomorphic to the coset action onf G:H, H = G_x. Normal subgroup is where left coset = right coset, subgroup is stable under conjugation of any element in g, or closed under conjugation. https://arxiv.org/pdf/1506.08376, one liner proof of weak , go back and digest this again. Let the domain R be a polynomial ring of n-many a_i generators over field K. Then every generator a_i is algebraic over K (Zariski's lemma). If K is algebraically cosed, then a_i is in K for all i. Without loss, by induction suppose a_1 not algebraic over K, then the remaining are in K. Each a_i is algebraic over K[a_1], implies polys f_2(a_1) ... f_n(a_1) in K[a_1] such that each a_i is integral over the domain A = K[a_1][1/f_n(a_1), ... 1/f_n(a_1)] i.e. form field extensions using inverses of polynomials of f_n(a_1) using field extension over K[a_1], thsi is similar to Liouville's theorem, R is integral over A, A is a field (using F is integral over subdomain D, then D is a field), so A = K(a_1) = K[a_1][1/f_n(a_1), ... 1/f_n(a_1)] which is absurd, (D is any PID with infinitely many primes, field of fractions is never a finitely generated D-algebra or K(a_1) is not a finitely generated K[a_1]-algebra). The prototype is that Q is not a finitely generated Z algebra. Replace all K with Q in the proof.

McKay grpahs are just undirected graphs with labelled dimension. These ideas are simple when G is compact subgroup of SU(2). Theorem by McKay, G is nontrivial compact subgroup of SU(2), pi is 2D rep, form McKay graph, vertices of graph is irred rho of G, vertex is label by deimensions, vertex rho and tau joined by m edges if tau appears with multiplicity m in rho otimes pi. Base point vertex 1 exists, harmonic, each label is twice the sum of adjacent labels, graph is connected, no loops no edge connects vertex to itself. Proof, (1) is trivial rep, part (2) is computation, part (3) is ever rep appears asas tensor power of pi, consequence of pi being faithful and self-dual and Stone-Weierstrass approximation theorem, (4) says rho cannot occur in rho otimes pi i.e. no loops.Labelled graph satisfying McKay's Theorem is harmonic graph, which are precisely graphs of closed subgroups of SU(2). Family mean some projective surjective map f from X to Y, Y is small, fibres of f are varying families parameterised by f, impose flatness or tensor product exact sequence to ensure fibres do not jump too much. Homotopy theory is the case where every map is a fibration, algebraic world means almost all maps will have singularities. Recall that blow ups when projected down give maps of singularities (not sure where I learnt this). Smooth maps is what topologists call submersion, any smooth family will be diffeomorphic. Sheaf is a local system if locally constant and finitely generated, can check that it is a category (subcategory of sheaves). Local systems are very different from vector bundles, U be subset over vector bundle, trivial, automorphisms are GL_n(O_U), n is rank of vector bundle, but local system is trivial over connected set U, autos are GL_n(k), smaller group. Local system is vector bundle with distinguished notion of a flat section. Exercise becomes obvious since Loc(X,k) is abelian subcategory of Sh(X,k) since Loc(X,k) is a special version of vector bundles. Category of local systems on X is equivalent to the abelian category of representations on finitely generated k-modules for X connected and x in X is a basepoint Rep(pi_1(X,x), k). Canonical example of constructible sheaf: f from X to Y is morphism of algebraic variety, consider sheaf on Y associated to presheaf U -> H^i(f^{-1}(U), k). A sheaf is constructible if stratification exists so that it is a local system witn singulartiies. But the title of local system with singularities is best reserved for intersection cohomology complexes. Complexes good, cohomology bad, study a complex up to quasiisomorphism than its cohomology groups. Start with Poincare duality, Verdier duality is a local theory of Poincare duality needed to work with exceptional pushforward and pullback with compact support to get sheaf and cosheaf duality.

Curves of genus 1 is different from elliptic curves One can assign j-invariant to curve of genus 1. B irred varieties, pi, X -> B is family of irreducible varieties. X seems to be a collection of elliptic curves, fundamentla group _1(B_sm, b) acts on cohomology H^k(p^{-1}(b), G) or fibres with coefficient group the abelian group G. image of pi_1 in the automorphism group of the cohomology gorup is called the monodromy group. Continuity on f is local, if continuous on sufficiently small open, then continuous on whole of X, to formalise local property use sieve, a set of opens is s seive if for every open in the set and every subopen inclusion of the open, we have this subopen in this set. This sieve is a covering if it is union of the opens belonging to it equals (why not overcover) to the full space X. Sieve is generated by a family opens. A property is local if property is true on opens iff property is true on subopen inclusions. Sieve point on view, use Cech point of view, let Z be coproduct of basis of opens for sieve, give function sieve-locally give function constant on the fibres of the natural projection of the coproduct on the topological space, the continuous functions form a sheaf. Now for stacks, instead of form a coproduct of opens, a sieve is generated by a family of opens, giving a vector bundle sieve-locally means giving a vector bundle E_i (total space) on each subopen inclusion generated basis elements of the family (basis elements, I am just going to call them subopens), an intersections of subopens gives a fibre product, an isomorphism of total spaces such that for Cech cocycles of triple fibre products, the relevant diagram commutes. So in a sense, stacks (or champs) means we are trying to make bundles work locally and glue them togethe. So a notion of a vector bundle is local like continuity being local. Zariski topology is not fine enough for the study of nonlinear problem, the above gives the Zariski topology.
